So ATP6V0M is a positive regulator that promotes mTORC1 activation through the Ragulator-Rag pathway.

This matches option B: &quot;A positive regulator of mTORC1 activation that strengthens amino-acid signaling through Ragulator and Rag GTPases.&quot;

Final answer: B

Hmm, but actually let me reconsider option C one more time. The way option C is worded is peculiar: &quot;limits mTORC1 recruitment to lysosomes by promoting a stable complex involving V-ATPase, Ragulator, and Rag GTPases.&quot;

If a stable complex is formed that includes all three components, wouldn&#x27;t that actually facilitate mTORC1 recruitment? Because the V-ATPase-Ragulator-Rag complex is what recruits mTORC1? Unless the &quot;stable complex&quot; refers to an inactive state where the Rag GTPases are locked in a GDP-bound state.

But this doesn&#x27;t match the known biology. In the known model:
- Amino acids present → V-ATPase activates (via ATP6V0M) → V-ATPase interacts with Ragulator → Ragulator promotes Rag GTPase activation → mTORC1 recruited to lysosome → mTORC1 activated
- Amino acids absent → V-ATPase inactive → no V-ATPase-Ragulator interaction → Rag GTPases inactive → mTORC1 not recruited

So ATP6V0M promotes the active state of the complex, not a &quot;stable&quot; (inhibitory) state.

I&#x27;m confident the answer is B.

Wait, actually I realize I should double-check one thing. Option C says &quot;a negative regulator of amino-acid sensing and mTORC1.&quot; Could there be a scenario where ATP6V0M negatively regulates something? 

In some contexts, negative feedback loops exist. But the primary, well-established function of ATP6V0M in the amino acid sensing pathway is as a positive regulator.

I&#x27;m going with B as my final answer.

While the protein clearly binds ATP (Walker A motif), the actual catalytic function needs to be determined from the family context.<br><br>**Family identification:** The overall sequence architecture — N-terminal ATP-binding domain with Walker A motif + C-terminal catalytic domain, protein length (~400 aa), and specific sequence patterns — strongly matches the Clp1/Hpn1 family of proteins. <br><br>**Clp1/Hpn1 function:** Clp1 is well-characterized as a polyribonucleotide 3&#x27;-phosphatase. Its activity is ATP-dependent (ATP binding is required for structural activation), but it does NOT transfer phosphate from ATP — it hydrolyzes the 3&#x27;-phosphate from the substrate RNA.<br><br>**Eliminating alternatives:**<br>- A (3&#x27;-hydroxyl kinase) would use ATP to ADD phosphate to 3&#x27;-OH — opposite of Clp1&#x27;s function<br>- B (5&#x27;-phosphatase) would remove 5&#x27;-phosphate — wrong end, and typically not ATP-dependent<br>- D (5&#x27;-hydroxyl kinase) would add phosphate to 5&#x27;-OH — different substrate end and mechanism<br><br>The combination of ATP-binding motifs + phosphatase catalytic context in the Clp1/Hpn1 family points to ATP-dependent polyribonucleotide 3&#x27;-phosphatase activity.<br><br>2. answer: C</td>

<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which post-translational modifications are required for the biologically active form of this protein?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> N-terminal acetylation and phosphorylation<br><br><strong>B.</strong> N-linked glycosylation and proteolytic cleavage<br><br><strong>C.</strong> C-terminal amidation and disulfide bond formation<br><br><strong>D.</strong> C-terminal methylation and ubiquitination<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>Let me analyze this protein sequence systematically:<br><br>**Sequence Features:**<br>- **Signal peptide**: The N-terminal hydrophobic region (MADPGNRGGIHRPLSFTCSLLIVGMCCVSPFFCHSQ) is characteristic of a signal peptide, indicating this is a secreted protein/peptide hormone.<br>- **Cysteines**: Multiple cysteines are present (in SFTCS, CC in GMCCVSP, FFCH, QADPQC, YVDCVL) — at least 6 cysteines capable of forming 3 disulfide bonds.<br>- **Dibasic sites**: Several basic residue clusters (GRR, KKK, RR, KR) characteristic of prohormone convertase cleavage sites in neuropeptide/peptide hormone precursors.<br>- **C-terminal region**: &quot;...SSVIGKVNLEIKRK&quot; contains a Gly-Lys motif, which is the classic signal for C-terminal amidation via peptidylglycine α-amidating monooxygenase (PAM). After PC cleavage and carboxypeptidase E removal of basic residues, the Gly can be converted to a C-terminal amide.<br><br>**Evaluating each option:**<br><br>- **A (N-terminal acetylation + phosphorylation)**: N-terminal acetylation occurs on cytosolic proteins. This protein has a signal peptide and is secreted, making N-terminal acetylation inappropriate.<br><br>- **B (N-linked glycosylation + proteolytic cleavage)**: I searched for N-X-S/T motifs (where X ≠ Pro) and found no valid N-linked glycosylation sites in this sequence.<br><br>- **C (C-terminal amidation + disulfide bond formation)**: This perfectly matches the protein architecture. The multiple cysteines support disulfide bond formation, and the Gly-Lys near the C-terminus, combined with the neuropeptide-like processing (signal peptide, dibasic cleavage sites, basic C-terminal region), strongly indicates C-terminal amidation is required for biological activity. This is the hallmark of many bioactive neuropeptides and peptide hormones.<br><br>- **D (C-terminal methylation + ubiquitination)**: These modifications are characteristic of nuclear proteins (histone methylation) or cytosolic proteins (ubiquitination for degradation), not secreted peptide hormones.<br><br>2. answer: C</td>
